ARB Identification of Diesel Emissions as a Toxic 
Air Contaminant 
In the early 1980s, the ARB established one of the nation’s 
first comprehensive state air toxics programs — the Cali- 
fornia Air Toxics Program. Its goal is to protect public 
health by reducing toxic air emissions that pose the 
highest risk to Californians. This requires two separate 
steps. During the first step, risk assessment, the ARB 
identifies the highest risk substances called toxic air 
contaminants. In the second step, risk management, the 
ARB and local air pollution control districts investigate 
and adopt measures requiring air toxics sources to mini- 
mize risk to public health. 
There are approximately 200 substances on the TAC list. 
More than 30 of these are found in diesel exhaust. On 
August 27, 1998, the TAC list was expanded to include 
particulate emissions from diesel engine exhaust, culmi- 
nating a near-decade long scientific investigation into the 
health effects of exposure to the fine particles and other 
pollutants in diese] exhaust. Similar to the findings of the 
MATES II study, the ARB identification of diese] exhaust 
particulate matter (as a surrogate for all diesel exhaust 
emissions) as a TAC, provides another driving force for the 
AQMD to pursue the development of a refuse truck fleet 
rule as a strategy to mitigate public exposure to this 
pollutant. 
Refuse Collection Vehicle Operations - General De- 
scription 
Waste hauling companies are regulated by local city and 
county governments. Section 40059 of the Public Re- 
sources Code specifically delegates state authority in this 
116 
area 
to local government agencies. Local governments 
provide the public with refuse collection services utilizing 
their own vehicle fleet and or they permit a private com- 
pany to provide this service in their area of jurisdiction. 
The vast majority of refuse collection services in the 
District are provided by private companies. Based on the 
best available information, only 19 local agencies utilized 
their own fleet vehicles to provide this service. Specific 
waste collection services provided by public or private 
fleets that are affected by PR1193 include (1) municipal 
solid waste collection to landfills, (2) recyclable materials 
collection, and (3) green waste collection. 
Private companies are allowed to provide refuse collection 
services through a contract or franchise with local jurisdic- 
tion, in return for rate regulation. Alternatively, the local 
jurisdiction may. permit a number of waste haulers to 
compete for business within that jurisdiction. Typically, 
services for residential solid waste collection ure permitted 
by exclusive contract or franchise, whereas commercial 
accounts are serviced by a number of permitted waste 
hauling companies that compete with each other. 
In general, the transfer of solid waste can be either from 
the source directly to a landfill, or from the source to a 
transfer station (e.g., a materials recovery facility) and 
finally to the landfill. The transfer station is an intermedi- 
ary location where curbside waste 
collection 
vehicles 
deposit their solid waste, and larger trucks, with much 
greater load capacity, transport solid waste from the 
transfer station to the landfill. Both transfer stations and 
landfills are either publicly or privately owned. 
Because the waste hauling industry is regulated by local 
city and county governments, no single government listing 


[PDF page 69]
117 
has been compiled of refuse companies offering services 
throughout the District. Nevertheless, based on industry 
input, staff currently estimates that there may be ap- 
proximately 6000 refuse trucks affected by PR1193. In an 
effort to refine this number, staff has surveyed local 
governments to provide the District with a list of waste 
hauling companies permitted to operate in their jurisdic- 
tion. To date, staff has compiled a list of approximately 
300 waste hauling companies operating in the District, 
which was expected based on input from the waste hauling 
industry. Staff has surveyed these companies directly to 
obtain a profile of their fleet population and related data, 
such as fuel consumption and average vehicle life, that 
could help estimate the emissions benefit of the rule. (See 
Attachment 2, Refuse Vehicle Usage Survey Form.) 
There are two basic chassis/engine combinations used for 
refuse trucks. The first type, for waste hauler use for 
curbside refuse collection or roll-off (refuse trucks that 
carry large bins to and from commercial sites) are typically 
of a GVW between 50,000 and 60,000 lb., and are gener- 
ally powered by heavy-duty engines in the range of about 
280 to 330 horsepower. These types of refuse trucks, based 
on industry input, constitute about 95 percent of all waste 
hauling vehicles. The second basic type of waste hauling 
vehicle is called a transfer vehicle. These resemble the 
largest line-hau! trucks, in that they are designed with a 
GVW of up to 80,000 lb. and are powered by heavy-duty 
engines of about 400 hp. These vehicles specialize in 
transferring solid waste from transfer facilities to the 
landfills. 
118 
Current Use of Natural Gas and Dual-Fuel Refuse 
Trucks 
Characteristics of a Natural Gas Waste Hauler. Natural 
gas refuse trucks can be powered by engines operated on 
LNG or CNG, or by dual-fuel engines that use both diesel 
fuel and natural gas. The natural gas fuel tanks add 
approximately 500 to 1500 lb. of weight to a refuse truck, 
which affects its payload capacity. Natural gas refuse 
truck engines are typically rated at 250 to 400 hp. 
Although the cost of natural gas is generally less than that 
for diesels, natural gas usage is up to 20% greater when 
compared to diesel usage. However, one enginé manufac- 
turer claims equivalent fuel usage for their diesel and 
natural gas engines. In addition, elimination of alternative 
emission control devices on diesel heavy-duty engines may 
narrow the fuel economy gap as well, per the 1998 settle- 
ment agreement between ARB, U.S. EPA and the seven 
HDE manufacturers. 
Attachment 3 lists information 
on the availability of 
natural gas engines and engine/chassis combinations that 
could be used in waste hauling vehicles. Attachment 4 lists 
the corresponding emissions related information for these 
engines. Engine manufactures producing dedicated natu- 
ral gas engines for waste hauling applications include 
Cummins Engine Company, Deere Power Systems, Detroit 
Diesel Corporation, and Mack Truck Corporation. Certified 
conversion systems for Caterpillar electronic diese] en- 
gines are available from Caterpillar dealers. These conver- 
sion systems convert the diesel engine into a compression 
ignition natural gas engine (commonly known as a “dual- 
fuel” or “bi-fuel” engine. Chassis manufacturers currently 
offering or planning to offer refuse trucks utilizing these 


[PDF page 70]
119 
engines are Crane Carrier, Freightliner Trucks, Mack 
Truck Corporation, Peterbilt, Western Star, and Alterna- 
tive Fuel Technology (AFT). A number of these refuse 
trucks and engines are currently operating in the District. 
These include natural gas waste hauling operations in 
Orange, Riverside, and LA counties. Waste Management of 
the Desert in Palm Desert and Waste Management of 
Orange County use spark ignited natural gas engines in 
their refuse trucks, while Taormina in Anaheim uses 
compression ignited natural gas/diese] dual-fuel refuse 
and transfer trucks. 
Based 
on industry input and staff observations, 
the 
operation of spark ignited natural gas powered refuse 
trucks has been successfully demonstrated from an overall 
operational standpoint. This has been specifically reported 
by Waste Management to District staff, based on their 
experience natural gas powered refuse trucks 
in the 
District and in other areas of the country. Within the 
District, Waste Management operates 30 natural 
gas 
powered refuse trucks in Coachella Valley (Waste Man- 
agement of the Desert) and in Orange County. Natural gas 
powered refuse trucks have been successfully demon- 
strated to the point where Waste Management plans to 
convert their entire fleets, operating in the Coachella 
Valley to using natural gas. 
The advantage of compression ignition natural gas over 
spark ignition natural gas are: a lower weight penalty 
from fuel tanks due to the superior fuel efficiency, less 
space required for fuel tanks due to the superior fuel 
efficiency, longer range due to the better fuel efficiency, 
lower capital cost due to fewer fuel tanks and lower capital 
cost due to the use of a standard base engine rather than a 
custom built spark ignition engine. 
120 
GENERAL DESCRIPTION AND EXPLANATION OF 
RULE REQUIREMENTS 
Purpose of the Rule 
The purpose of PR 1193 is to reduce criteria pollutants 
(NOx) and toxic (PM) emissions from municipal solid 
waste collection operations in the District. This is accom- 
plished by requiring purchasers of fleet refuse trucks, 
when adding or replacing heavy-duty refuse trucks to their 
fleets, to purchase cleaner-burning, lower-emitting, alter- 
native-fuel 
or dual-fuel 
heavy-duty 
refuse 
trucks, 
as 
commercially. available. Alternative 
fuel is defined 
as 
compressed or liquefied natural gas, liquefied petroleum 
gas, methanol, electricity, fuel cells, or other advanced 
technology that does not rely on diesel fuel. Dual-fuel 
vehicles are equipped with a diesel engine that uses an 
alternative fuel in combination with the diesel fuel to 
enable compression ignition. 
Applicability 
The proposed rule applies to public agencies and private 
companies that operate solid waste collection vehicles and 
their fleets consist of 15 or more solid waste collection 
(vehicles that operate on fixed routes and collects solid 
waste 
at 
residential 
or 
commercial 
establishments), 
transfer, or rolloff vehicles. PR1193 maximizes the air 
quality benefit for this fleet segment by taking advantage 
of the significant benefits of natural gas engine technology 
in terms of its inherently low NOx and PM emissions. In 
an effort to facilitate the transition from diesel to dedi- 
cated natural gas operation, PR1193 allows compression 
ignited dual-fuel technology for a limited time (prior to 
July 1, 2002) for purchases of solid waste collection 


[PDF page 71]
121 
vehicles. However, dual-fuel vehicle purchases or leases is 
provided for transfer or rolloff vehicles with no time 
limitations. The time restriction is placed on the solid 
waste collection vehicles so that AQMD staff can futher 
[sic] evaluate the emission benefits of the dual-fuel tech- 
nology in the solid waste collection duty cycle. Unlike the 
other vehicles where dual-fuel is allowed with no time 
limitations, these vehicles may use natural gas for a 
smaller part of their duty cycle and may not be as low 
emitting as in other duty cycles. As part of this evaluation, 
AQMD staff intends to evaluate all engine technologies as 
they are applied in the refuse collection operations. 
For the purposes of PR1193, a heavy-duty truck is a 
vehicle having a GVW of at least 14,000 pounds. A refuse 
truck is one that is used for the express purpose of collect- 
ing and/or transporting solid waste, except hazardous 
waste, as defined in Section 40191l(a) of the Public Re- 
sources code. 
Vehicle Purchase Requirements 
The proposed rule would require that beginning July 1, 
2001, public and affected private operators of fleets con-. 
sisting of 50 or more solid waste collection vehicles and 
beginning July 1, 2002, for public and private operators 
with 15 or more solid waste collection vehicles or a com- 
bined total of 15 or more rolloff, transfer, or solid waste 
~ collection vehicles, when adding or replacing a heavy-duty 
refuse truck to their fleets, to purchase or lease an alter- 
native-fuel 
heavy-duty 
refuse 
truck, 
as commercially 
available. In addition, the rule requirements would pro- 
vide, the option of purchasing or leasing any solid waste 
collection vehicle having a dual-fuel engine that has been 
122 
ARB-certified to meet an optional NOx standard and a 
particulate emissions level equivalent to an alternative- 
fuel engine. Beginning July 1, 2001, for public or private 
operators with a combined total of 15 or more transfer or 
rolloff vehicles, all new purchases of transfer or rolloff 
vehicles must be equipped with an alternative-fueled or a 
dual-fuel engine. Vehicles that are contracted for purchase 
prior to the rule-effectiveness date are not required to 
comply with PR1193 purchase requirements. However, 
options to purchase vehicles exercised after the adoption of 
Rule 1193 would be subject to the rule requirements. 
Permitted alternative fuels include compressed or lique- 
fied natural gas, propane, methanol, electricity, fuels cells 
or other advanced technologies that do not rely on diesel 
fuel, consistent with allowable alternative fuels in ARB 
transit bus regulation. For the purposes of this rule, a 
dual-fuel engine is one that uses a combination of natural 
gas and diesel fuel, as described in the above sections. 
Exemptions 
In response to comment from entities potentially affected 
by the proposed 
rule, PR1193 exempts certain types 
vehicles purchased from the utilization of alternative 
fuels. These include evaluation/test vehicles, vehicles not 
used for the express purpose of collecting and transporting 
solid waste from residential or commercial establishments 
to transfer stations or landfills, and alternative fuel model 
unavailability. 
These exemptions 
allow 
for promising 
experimental technologies to be demonstrated, clarify the 
intent of the rule, and address concerns regarding the 
possibility that a satisfactory alternative-fuel refuse truck 
may not be available for a specific niche application. 


[PDF page 72]
123 
In addition, under emergency situation such as a natural 
disaster, conventionally-fueled refuse collection vehicles 
may be brought into the District as allowed under the 
AQMD Rule 118 if the Governor or President declares a 
state of emergency. 
Comments were made that for smaller operators there 
may still be a need to provide some relief from the rule 
requirements if the infrastructure or funding for alterna- 
tive fuel vehicles are not available. PR 1193 now provides 
language that the operator may make a case before the 
AQMD Hearing Board as provided in AQMD Regulation V 
to seek relief from the rule requirements, in some cases. 
Persons in violation of this rule are subject to all penalties 
and enforcement proceedings specified in the Health and 
Safety Code until such time as they obtain a variance from 
the District Hearing Board. 
Compliance Auditing and Enforcement 
PR1193 will require that affected public agencies keep 
sufficient vehicle data records to document rule compli- 
ance, and that these records be maintained for a minimum 
of two years. The AQMD intends to audit these records, 
either at the vehicle fleet location or by requesting appro- 
priate documents to be submitted to the AQMD for review. 
The specific data to be kept for each new vehicle will 
include the DMV Certificate of Title and registration, 
vehicle manufacturer, model-year, model, engine family 
number, fuel type, and fuel usage. In addition, to the 
preceding, affected fleets utilizing the exemption provision 
of the proposed rule must supply documentation proving 
the exemption of each exempt vehicle purchase to AQMD 
124 
upon request. If a public agency is found to be in non- 
compliance with rule requirements, then the public agency 
will be subject to penalties specified in Health and Safety 
Code Division 26, Part 4, Chapter 4, Article 3. The AQMD 
also plans to develop an enforcement guideline document 
that will stress the implementation of corrective actions by 
public fleets rather than punitive monetary penalties 
during the initial years of rule implementation, for first 
time violators. 
EMISSION BENEFITS 
Criteria Pollutants 
The goal of the proposed fleet rule is to reduce PM and 
NOx emissions 
through 
the introduction 
and use 
of 
cleaner-burning 
vehicles. 
These emission 
benefits 
are 
expected to primarily consist of reduced toxic exposure to 
diesel particulate matter and reduced emissions of nitro- 
gen oxides. With regard to the criteria pollutants, PR1193 
is specifically based on achieving emission reductions 
beyond the mandatory U.S. EPA and ARB heavy-duty 
engine emission standards. 
The emission 
benefits 
for 
PR1193 
are based 
on 
refuse truck 
fleets purchasing 
alternative-fueled refuse trucks instead of diese] refuse 
trucks. 
Emission reduction estimates were originally developed 
for the entire suite of fleet rules using an approach based 
on vehicle-miles-traveled (VMT) data from the latest ARB- 
approved emissions inventory model, EMFAC7G. Based on 
this approach and on general assumptions that apply to all 
heavy-duty vehicles in all of the proposed fleet rules, 
estimates for the year 2010 would be 11 to 16 tons per year 
of PM and 75 tons per year of NOx. 


[PDF page 73]
125 
126

8. PM standard for diesel HDEs is based on 0.1 g/bhp-hr 
emission standard.

level of 0.03 g/bhp-hr is assumed, based on ARB input 
and certification data for HDEs. Also, this emission 
level is consistent with the PM standard for the alter- 
native-fuel path in the recently adopted ARB Urban 
Bus Fleet Rule.

Air Toxics 
Esti 
| Relative 
‘Toxicity 
of Diesel and Natural 
G 
Powered Refuse Trucks. 
The relative air toxic risks of diesel and corresponding 
natural gas refuse trucks were estimated using an ap- 
proach based on determining weighted toxic risk factors 
for the two fuels under consideration. The weighted toxic 
risk factor is determined by multiplying the individual 
toxic constituents of the exhaust by their respective cancer 
potency factor, and then proportionately adjusting these 
values by an estimated annual mass emission rate of 
particulate matter (PM) and non-methane hydrocarbon 
emissions (NMHC). The purpose of this analysis is to use 
these weighted toxicity factors to estimate the number of 
natural gas refuse trucks roughly equivalent to one diesel 
refuse trucks [sic] based on toxic risk. 
For the purposes of this analysis, the toxic component 
analyzed for diesel refuse trucks is limited to total PM 
emissions. This is because ARB staff has indicated that 
the toxic risk factor for diesel PM already incorporates 
toxic risks from all other constituents in diesel exhaust. 
For natural gas refuse trucks, the relative toxic risk was 
estimated based on the PM contribution of nickel and 
hexavalent chromium emissions, and the NMHC emis- 
sions of formaldehyde, acetaldehyde, benzene, and 1,3 
butadiene. ARB speciation profiles were used to develop 
nickel and hexavalent fraction of the natural gas PM 
exhaust. With regard to NMHC components, a paper from 
West Virginia University (SAE paper 972971) was used to 
develop the benzene and 1,3 butadiene NMHC fractions, 
and an ARB speciation profile from an industrial natural 
gas-powered 
internal combustion engine was used 
to 
develop 
the 
formaldehyde 
and 
acetaldehyde 
NMHC 


[PDF page 75]
129 
fractions. (The West Virginia University paper provided 
speciation data generated from a CNG-powered engine 
used in on-road vehicle applications, but did not specifi- 
cally include formaldehyde and acetaldehyde data.) 
For the purposes of this specific analysis, the annual PM 
emission rates for diesel and natural gas refuse trucks 
were developed using the same assumptions contained in 
the criteria pollutant benefit methodology. These assump- 
tions include diese] refuse truck emissions of 0.1 g/bhp-hr 
for 2000 and subsequent years, and natural gas refuse 
truck emissions of 0.03 g/bhp-hr for 2000 and beyond. The 
annual mass emission rate of NMHC emissions for natura! 
gas engines is highly variable based on input received by 
engine manufacturers, as evidenced by ARB certification 
data for natural gas engine families approved for sale in 
California. For the purposes of this analysis, a range of 
NMHC emissions was estimated using this certification 
data. Using this range, which corresponds to 0.3 g/bhp-hr 
to 0.8 g/bhp-hr, for the 2000-to-9/2002 time period and 0.3 
to 0.5 g/bhp-hr for the 10/2002-and-later time period, 
assumed conversion factor of 18.5 bhp-hr/gal for refuse 
trucks, and an assumed fuel consumption for refuse trucks 
of 10,000 gal/yr, annual NMHC emissions were deter- 
mined. 
Table 4 shows the annual PM and NMHC mass emission 
rates, relative toxicity factors for PM and NMHC exhaust 
components, 
and the overall weighted toxicity factor 
Based on these overall weighted toxicity factors, Table 5 
shows the number of CNG refuse trucks that is roughly 
equivalent to one corresponding diesel-powered refuse 
trucks [sic]. The number is equal to the overall weighted 
toxicity factor for the diese] refuse trucks divided by the 
corresponding value for the natura] gas refuse trucks. 
130 
Different time frames are utilized in this analysis to 
account fr more strimgent emission standards for NOx, 
and NMHC that are implemented in the overall time 
frame being analyzed. 
Based on this analysis, it can be concluded that significant 
toxic emission benefits will occur on a per vehicle basis 
from the use on a natural gas refuse truck versus a diesel 
refuse trick. Depending on the time frame, one diesel 
refuse truck is estimated to have the same toxicity as up to 
74 corresponding natural gas refuse trucks, as shown in 
Table 5. Depending on the availability of particulate traps 
that are still under development by contro] device and 
engine manufacturers, 
this ratio may be expected to 
decrease.

COST ANALYSIS 
Fleet Cost Impacts 
Increased capital and operational cost associated with 
compliance with the proposed rule is expected. However, 
individual cost impacts will vary depending on the indi- 
vidual public agency or private fleet. Cost impacts may be 
incurred 
through 
increased 
purchase, 
maintenance, 
building 
upgrade, 
and 
refueling 
infrastructure 
costs. 
These impacts may be partially offset by lower fuel costs. 
The capital cost of a spark ignition natural gas vehicle is 
more expensive than a comparable diesel model by a 
differential of approximately $40,000 per truck whilc [sic] 
132 
compression ignition gas versions of sirnilar trucks have 
demonstrated a price differential of $25,000 to $30,000. 
This is primarily a result of the current low-volume 
production of natural gas vehicles. It is possible with 
larger production volumes and economies of scale, the 
capital cost is projected to decrease, based on an industry 
estimate, to about $25,000 per vehicle for a spark ignition 
natural gas vehicle in the near term. 
The maintenance costs of current CNG engines used in 
waste hauling operations are generally higher, but man- 
ageable, according to waste hauling industry representa- 
tives. It is expected that these maintenance costs will 
decrease 
as 
natural 
gas engine technology 
matures, 
according to natural gas heavy-duty engine industry 
input. As is common with new technologies, the mainte- 
nance costs for the first generation of natural gas vehicles 
are generally higher than for diesels. With increasing 
experience and training, maintenance issues have been 
resolved. At the same time, fleets have changed their 
preventative maintenance schedules to take advantage of 
the benefits of natural gas — especially the greatly in- 
creased oi] life and reduced engine wear. For example, 
John Deere advertises “diesel-like fuel economy, longer 
service intervals, easier servicing, less downtime, and 
longer engine life” with regard to natural gas powered 
heavy-duty engines. The result has been a steady reduc- 
tion in NGV maintenance costs. There have been numer- 
ous examples of fleets, in transit operations, that have 
experienced natural gas vehicle maintenance cost impacts 
that have been very comparable to their diese] counter- 
parts. Staff believes that fleets operating natural gas 
powered refuse trucks as well will experience these posi- 
tive results. 


[PDF page 77]
133 
For example, Pierce Transit ia Washington, with a fleet 
including many first and second generation CNG engines, 
has experienced total maintenance and oil costs of 16.4 
cents per mile on its CNG buses, compared to 16.2 cents 
per mile for its diesel buses. The Antelope Valley School 
district experienced average maintenance costs of 13 cents 
per mile on 16 CNG school buses equipped with Deere 
engines, compared to 21 cents per mile for diesel buses 
purchased about the same time, and 24 cents per mile for 
its existing diesel buses. For a fleet of 136 1993-1996 CNG 
buses, Sacramento Regional Transit District found its 
maintenance costs to be 18.1 cents per mile in 1997, 
compared to 27.7 cents per mile for its fleet of 73 1985- 
1990 diesel buses. Sunline Transit Agency in the Palm 
Springs area reported average maintenance costs of 18.4 
cents per mile for 34 1993-model CNG buses. Sunline has 
estimated that their overall costs have decreased by 27 
percent through the use [sic] compressed natural gas 
(CNG) urban buses compared to diesel-powered buses. 
The socioeconomic report prepared by the AQMD staff 
provides additional information relative to the costs and 
potential economic impacts of PR1193. Using the cost 
impacts ascribed to PR1193 and the emission benefits as 
detailed above based on best-available information, the 
cost-effectiveness ranges between $8,000 per ton of emis- 
sion reduction for the “best-case funding scenario” and 
$40,000 per ton of emission reduction for the worst-case 
funding scenario. The “most likely funding scenario” is 
$25,000 per ton of emission reduction. 
134 
Incremental Cost-Effectiveness Analysis 
Health and Safety Code Section 40920.6 requires an 
assessment of incremental cost effectiveness for proposed 
regulations relative to ozone, CO, SOx, NOx, and their 
precursors. Incremental cost effectiveness is defined as the 
difference in control costs divided by the difference in 
emission reductions between two potential control options 
that can achieve the same emission reduction goal of a 
regulation. Compared to Proposed Rule 1193, incremental 
cost effectiveness of including non-waste hauling vehicles 
into the vehicle count as a result of including fleets where 
the total number of vehicles is 15 or more, but do not have 
15 vehicles used in waste hauling activities, for determin- 
ing the proposed rule’s applicability is $28,900 per ton of 
combined pollutants (NOx and PM) assuming no funding. 
Approximately 
1,200 waste hauling vehicles and five 
natural gas refueling stations would be added as a result 
of the analysis. 
Funding Programs 
Various federal, state and local funding programis are 
available to assist public waste hauling agencies (and, in 
some programs, their private partners) in the acquisition 
and operation 
[sic] alternative-fuel 
heavy-duty 
refuse 
trucks. These are generally described below. In addition, a 
more 
detailed 
discussion 
of these funding sources 
is 
included in the socioeconomic report that is currently 
being prepared by AQMD staff. 
State Energy Program. States will promote the conserva- 
tion of energy, reduce the rate of growth of energy demand, 
and reduce dependence 
on imported 
oil through the 
development and implementation 
of a comprehensive 


[PDF page 78]
135 
State Energy Program. The State Energy Program is the 
result of the consolidation of two formula grant programs 
— the State Energy Conservation Program and the Institu- 
tional Conservation Program. The State Energy Program 
includes provisions for competitively awarded financial 
assistance for a number of state-oriented special project 
activities, 
including 
alternative 
fuels. 
In addition 
to 
funding for special project activities, states may choose to 
allocate base formula funds to programs to accelerate the 
use of alternative fuels for government vehicles. 
For more information, contact your State Energy Office or 
the DOE Regional Office for your region, listed under the 
Points of Contact section for your state, or contact Ron 
Santoro at DOE Headquarters at (202) 586-8296. 
The Carl Moyer Program was established by ARB to 
provide incentives to encourage and expedite implementa- 
tion of the cleanest commercially available vehicles, and 
specifically to reduce emissions from heavy-duty engines. 
The incentives are grants for offsetting the higher costs of 
primarily alternative-fuel heavy-duty vehicles, and for 
supporting 
the fueling infrastructure. 
The ARB has 
established overall program requirements and allocates 
funds to local air districts, including AQMD, for local 
program administration. To date, both dedicated natural 
gas and dual-fuel refuse trucks have been approved for 
funding by the Governing Board. 
The AQMD contact is Cindy Sullivan (AQMD) at 909-396- 
3249. 
Mobile S 
Lir Pollution Reduction Review C 
ao 
(MSRC) Discretionary Funds, This annual work program, 
that typically includes an HDV incentive program, pays 
136 
the incremental cost for the purchase of new OEM alter- 
native-fuel engines and vehicles. 
Thirty percent of the funds collected each year from a $4 
surcharge on vehicle registration created by AB 2766 
(Sher) goes to the Mobile Source Air Pollution Reduction 
Review Committee (MSRC) to be used to implement 
programs to reduce mobile source emissions. Managers of 
the program have apportioned the available funding into 
several technology-specific categories, including alterna- 
tive-fuel heavy-duty refuse trucks. To date, both dedicated 
natural gas and dual-fuel refuse trucks have been ap- 
proved for MSRC Program funding by the Governing 
Board. 
The AQMD contact is Ray Gorski (MSRC Technical Advi- 
sor) at 909-396-2479. 
Local Government Subvention Funds. Forty percent of the 
AB 2766 funds collected go to local governments based on 
a pro-rated share of population and must be used to reduce 
mobile source emissions. Cities can use their funds to 
purchase alternative-fuel vehicles or engines. While these 
funds are used primarily by municipalities for their own 
projects, these monies can be allocated by the city for 
public-private partnerships to pursue AFV and EV pro- 
jects. Funds not expended carry over from year to year. 
The AQMD staff contacts are Larry Rhinehart (AQMD) at 
909-396-2898 and Oscar Abarca (AQMD) at 909-396-3242. 
Current and recent users and applicants of the above co-

in the District, and plans an additional 200 CNG and LNG 
trucks during the next two years, along with six new

and Yucaipa. Burrtec replaced 13 diesel trucks in last

Board does hereby direct staff to notify the Governing 
Board of any changes in state or federal law that may 
expand or clarify the authority of the AQMD to pursue a 
fuel neutral fleet rule policy and other policy changes as 
adopted, and to recommend revisions to the rule to be 
consistent with state or federal statutory requirements. 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that the Governing 
Board does hereby direct staff to work with shareholders 
to evaluate the possibility of expanding the compliance 
flexibility provisions of the rule to allow programs such as 
an exchange mechanism between various vehicle types 
(light, medium and heavy duty), and between fleets within 
the region. Within six months, staff shall report to the 
Governing Board with its findings and recommendations. 
146 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that the Governing 
Board does hereby direct the AQMD Technology Advance- 
ment Office to hold an annual symposium to review the 
research, development, and demonstration of advanced 
diesel technology, electric vehicles, hybrid vehicles, fuel 
cell powered vehicles, and other advanced vehicles and to 
include a summary of the findings in an annual report to 
the Governing Board. 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that the Governing 
Board does hereby direct staff to return to the Governing 
Board with proposed amendments to Rule 1192 to allow 
an exemption for the purchase of a conventionally fueled 
vehicle where the engine and control device have been 
approved by CARB to meet certification emission levels 
which achieve at least a 54 percent reduction in nitrogen 
oxide and a 71 percent reduction in particulate matter 
exhaust emissions from the currently available conven- 
tionally fueled engine (family) certification emission levels 
approved by CARB (as of the date of adoption of this rule); 
and which achieves a reduction in toxic risk equivalent to 
the level which would be achieved, on average, by cur- 
rently available alternative fueled engines certified by 
CARB. Equivalent toxic risk shall be calculated based 
upon unit risk factors as approved by the California Office 
of Environmental Health Hazard Assessment (OEHHA). 
Procedures for calculating the toxic risk reduction shall be 
developed in consultation with CARB and OEHHA, within 
90 days of the date of adoption of this rule and presented 
to the Governing Board as an informational item. Staff 
shall propose such rule amendments within 90 days of 
receipt of sufficient technical information establishing that 
the above criteria have been satisfied. 


[PDF page 84]
147 
The Governing Board also directs staff to periodically 
review the above guidance relative to technology advance- 
ment and to report back to the Governing Board at such 
time as changes may be appropriate. 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that the Governing 
Board does hereby request CARB to consider the feasibil- 
ity of further enhancing its heavy-duty engine and urban 
bus certification procedures to better reflect in-use emis- 
sions over representative duty cycles. 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that when CARB 
updates its heavy-duty engine and urban bus certification 
procedures, that the AQMD will utilize such procedures 
relative to Rule 1192. 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, based on the find- 
ings in the annual progress report described above, staff 
may recommend modifications to the rule requirements for 
the Governing Board’s consideration. 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that the Governing 
Board does hereby adopt, pursuant 
to the authority 
granted by law, Proposed Rule 1192 - Clean On-Road 
Transit Buses, as set forth in the attached and incorpo- 
rated herein by reference.

Plaintiff ENGINE MANUFACTURERS 
ASSOCIA- 
TION (“EMA”), by and through its attorneys, and pursu- 
ant to Local Rule 7.14.1, hereby submits this Statement of 
Uncontroverted Facts and Conclusions of Law in support 
of EMA’s Motion for Summary Judgment. 
Uncontroverted Material Facts 
EMA contends that there is no genuine issue with 
respect to any of the following material facts: 
A. Background Facts 
1. This case centers on six regulations (Rules 1191- 
1194, 1186.1 and 1196) relating to the control of emissions 
from certain fleets of motor vehicles operating within the 
South Coast Air Basin. These regulations are generally 
referred to as the “SCAQMD Fleet Rules.” The South 
Coast Air Quality Management District (“SCAQMD”), 
acting through its Board members and executive officer 
(collectively, the “SCAQMD Defendants”) adopted the 
SCAQMD Fleet Rules pursuant to Board hearings held on 
June 16, 2000, August 18, 2000, and October 20, 2000. 
(Complaint and Answer, {{ 1, 47.) 


[PDF page 92]
161 
2. Defendant SCAQMD is the air quality manage- 
ment district established under the California Health and 
Safety Code to develop and implement a strategy for 
achieving and maintaining ambient air quality standards 
within the South Coast Air Basin. Agents of the SCAQMD, 
including the SCAQMD Defendants, are responsible for 
administering the Fleet Rules at issue. (Complaint and 
Answer, { 14.) 
3. EMA is the not-for-profit trade association that 
represents the leading manufacturers of internal combus- 
tion engines used in truck and bus applications. One of 
EMA’s principal purposes is to represent the interests of 
its member companies in regulatory proceedings, and, 
where necessary, to challenge rulemaking activities that 
are in violation of the underlying statutes. Since the 
SCAQMD Fleet Rules will prohibit or limit the purchase 
(and, therefore, 
sale) of EMA’s members’ diesel-fueled 
engine products, EMA’s members are subject to and will be 
adversely affected by the SCAQMD Fleet Rules. (Declara- 
tion of Jed Mandel; Ex. 1 to EMA’s Supporting Memoran- 
dum.) 
4. EMA has initiated this action for a declaratory 
judgment that the SCAQMD Fleet Rules are preempted 
and void under the federal Clean Air Act (“CAA”), and for 
injunctive 
relief against 
any 
efforts 
to enforce 
the 
SCAQMD Fleet Rules. 
5. The 
U.S. 
Environmental 
Protection 
Agency 
(“EPA”) and the State of California, acting through its Air 
Resources Board (“ARB”), have the authority to regulate 
mobile sources of air pollution (i.e. motor vehicles and 
motor vehicle engines), and are doing so in a comprehensive 
manner through a series of emission control regulations. 
162 
Other states 
and political subdivisions, 
however, 
are 
preempted under Section 209(a) of the CAA from “adopting 
or attempting to enforce any standard relating to the 
control of emissions” from new motor vehicles or motor 
vehicle engines. 42 U.S.C. § 7543(a) (Emphasis added.) 
Section 177 of the CAA sets forth additional express 
preemption provisions. See 42 U.S.C. § 7507. 
6. More 
specifically, 
Section 
209(a) 
of the CAA 
provides, as follows: 
(a) Prohibition 
No state or any political subdivision thereof 
shall adopt or attempt to enforce any standard 
relating to the control of emissions from new mo- 
tor vehicles or new motor vehicle engines subject 
to this part. No State [or political subdivision 
thereof] shall require certification, inspection, or 
any other approval relating to the control of 
emissions from: any new motor vehicle or new 
motor vehicle engine as condition precedent to 
the initial retail sale, titling (if any), or registra- 
tion of such motor vehicle, motor vehicle engine, 
or equipment. 42 U.S.C. §7543(a). 
7. 
Section 177 of the CAA provides in relevant part, 
as follows: 
Nothing in this section or in subchapter II of 
this chapter shall be construed as authorizing any 
such State to prohibit or limit, directly or indi- 
rectly, the manufacture or sale of a new motor ve- 
hicle or motor vehicle engine that is certified in 
California as meeting California standards, or to 
take any action of any kind to create, or have the 
effect of creating, a motor vehicle or motor vehicle 
engine different than a motor vehicle or engine 


[PDF page 93]
163 
certified in California under California standards 
(a “third vehicle”) or otherwise create such a 
“third vehicle.” 42 U.S.C. §7507. 
8. When Congress enacts legislation, it may lawfully 
prohibit parallel state legislation and occupy or preempt a 
particular field. (Complaint and Answer, {4 83, 90, 97, 
104, 111, 118.) 
9. One of Congress’ express purposes in enacting 
Sections 209%a) and 177 of the CAA was 
to protect 
manufacturers of motor vehicles and motor vehicle engines 
from a myriad of potentially inconsistent and burdensome 
state and local emission-control standards for new motor 
vehicles and new motor vehicle engines. (Complaint and 
Answer, J 6.) 
10. 
Over the past several years, EPA, and the CARB 
have adopted similar emission control requirements for 
new heavy-duty on-highway diesel-fueled motor vehicles 
and engines. These include current NOx and PM stan- 
dards of 4.0 g/bhp-hr and 0.1 g/bhp-hr (0.05 g/bhp-hr for 
urban buses), respectively, and, starting in 2004, a NOx + 
NMHC standard of 2.4 g/bhp-hr and a PM standard of . 
0.10 g/bhp-hr (0.05 g/bhp-hr for urban buses). (Complaint 
and Answer, {7 34-3! ) 
11. 
The ARB also has enacted separate, more strin- 
gent emission control programs for motor vehicles, includ- 
ing one for light and medium-duty vehicles, including 
certain diesel-fueled vehicles (the “LEV Program”), and 
one for heavy-duty urban transit buses (the “Urban Bus 
Program”). (Complaint and Answer, { 38.) 
12. 
The LEV Program requires manufacturers to 
produce and deliver for sale vehicles or engines certified to 
164 
various emission-based 
categories. 
The LEV Program 
achieves emission reductions by requiring manufacturers 
to sell progressively cleaner mixes of vehicles over time. 
(Complaint and Answer, { 39.) 
13. 
In the medium-duty 
vehicle 
category, 
which 
includes certain diesel-fueled pickup trucks, vans and 
large SUVs, the LEV Program achieves emissions reduc- 
tions by requiring all vehicles to be certified to more 
stringent emission categories in specified model years (z.e. 
LEV standards in 2002, and ULEV standards in 2004). 
Medium-duty vehicle manufacturers also have the flexibil- 
ity to balance the sale of higher emitting vehicles (e.g. 
vehicles certified to Tier 1 levels) with vehicles certified to 
more 
stringent 
emission 
requirements 
(e.g. 
vehicles 
certified to ULEV levels). (Complaint and Answer, { 41.) 
14. 
With respect to new medium-duty diesel-fueled 
motor vehicle engines, the LEV Program, in essence, 
allows for the sale of those engine products through the 
2002-2004 timeframe when the LEV and ULEV require- 
ments take effect. Consequently, new diesel-fueled me- 
dium-duty 
motor 
vehicle 
engines 
can 
be sold 
and 
purchased under the ARB’s LEV Program for at least the 
next 
several 
years. 
(Complaint 
and Answer, { 42.) 
(SR1191-17.) 
15. 
On February 24, 2000, the ARB adopted its 
Urban Bus Program. To implement this ARB regulation, 
urban transit bus fleet operators are required to choose 
between two different compliance paths — a “diesel path” 
or an “alternative-fuel path.” Fleet operators were re- 
quired to make this choice and notify ARB of their decision 
by January 31, 2001. (Complaint and Answer, { 43.) 


[PDF page 94]
165 
16. 
The ARB Urban Bus Program specifically per- 
mits the purchase and sale of diesel-fueled transit buses, 
both currently and into the future. (Complaint and An- 
swer, 7 45.) 
17. 
With respect to all other new heavy-duty on- 
highway diesel-fueled motor vehicles and engines (other 
than new urban transit buses), the governing emission 
control requirements, both nationally and in California, 
are those as described in paragraph 34 of EMA’s Amended 
Complaint (and in paragraph 10 above). Consequently, and 
as long as any medium-duty or heavy-duty on-highway 
diesel-fueled motor vehicle engine meets those specified 
emission requirements, it can be purchased and sold in 
California, '»h currently and into the future. (Complaint 
and Answer, { 46.) 
18. 
The SCAQMD Defendants have relied on Cali- 
fornia Health and Safety Code section 40447.5 in crafting 
and adopting the SCAQMD Fleet Rules. Health and Safety 
Code section 40447.5 was enacted in 1987, three years 
prior to the 1990 amendments to the CAA that added the 
specific “third vehicle” prohibitions to CAA Section 177. 
(Complaint and Answer, { 71.) 
B. Rule 1191 
19. 
Rule 1191, entitled “Clean On-Road Light-and 
Medium-Duty Public Fleet Vehicles,” was adopted on June 
16, 2000. A copy of Rule 1191 is appended hereto as Ex- 
hibit “A.” 
20. 
The stated purpose of Rule 1191 (as set forth in 
paragraph (a) of the Rule) is to require passenger car, 
light-duty truck, or medium-duty vehicle fleet operators to 
166 
acquire low-emitting gasoline and alternative-fuel vehicles 
to reduce air toxic and criteria pollutant emissions when 
procuring or leasing these vehicles in the District.” (Rule 
1191(a); emphasis added.) 
21. 
Paragraph (cX1) of Rule 1191 defines “alterna- 
tive-fueled vehicle” to mean “a vehicle or engine that is not 
powered by gasoline or diesel fuel and emits hydrocarbon, 
carbon monoxide, or nitrogen oxides, on an individual 
basis at least equivalent to or lower than a ULEV based on 
ARB’s certification data.” (1191(c\1); emphasis added.) 
22. 
Paragraph (d) of Rule 1191 provides that “[u]pon 
adoption of this rule and every six months thereafter, the 
Executive Officer shall publish a list of engines and 
associated 
vehicle 
models 
certified 
by California Air 
Resources Board (CARB) that fleet operators can choose 
from to comply with paragraph (e) of this Rule.” (Rule 
1191(d).) 
23. 
Paragraph (e1) of Rule 1191 provides that 
“(bjeginning July 1, 2001, public fleet operators of 15 or 
more vehicles shall procure from the list published by the 
Executive Officer as required under paragraph (d), or 
otherwise certified by CARB as equivalent low-emitting 
gasoline or alternative-fuel 
vehicles, when adding or 
replacing vehicles to their vehicle fleet.” (Rule 1191(e)(1).)

leum gas (propane), methanol, electricity, or fuel cells. 
Hybrid-electric and dual-fuel technologies that use diesel 
fuel are not considered alternative-fuel technologies for the 
purposes of this rule.” (Rule 1186.1(c)2); emphasis added.)

Paragraph (d)(1) of Rule 1186.1 provides that

alternative-fuel sweepers for each individual purchase or 
lease of any replacement or additional sweeper. (Rule 
1186.1(d)(1).)

The objective of these rules is to reduce ex- 
haust emissions from on-road vehicles and the 
public’s exposure to air toxic contaminants and 
criteria pollutants. (SR 1186.1-1.) 
* 
* 
* 
One of the primary objectives of the proposed 
Clean Fleets program, including PR 1186.1, is to 
reduce mobile source emissions, particularly die- 
sel particulates, by accelerating the implementa- 
tion of currently available alternative-fuel vehicle 
technology. (SR 1186.1-3.) 
* 
* 
* 
The purpose of PR 1186.1 is to increase the 
number of alternative-fuel sweepers used by fleet 
operators providing sweeping services 
in the 
AQMD’s jurisdiction to reduce air toxic and crite- 
ria pollutant emissions. (SR 1186.1-5.) 
62. 
The Staff Report for Rule 1186.1 confirms that 
the purchase and sale of certain ARB-certified diesel-fuel 
vehicles and engines will be prohibited and limited under 
the Rule. Specifically, the Staff Report states, 
180 
For the purposes of this rule, hybrid-electric 
and dual-fuel sweepers that use diesel fuel are not 
considered at this time to be powered by alterna- 
tive fuels. 
... Because sweeper manufacturers 
must typically remove the existing diesel engine 
and fuel system that the chassis/engine manufac- 
turers provide before they can install an alterna- 
tive-fuel engine and fuel system, there is an 
increased incremental cost for fleet operators 
who purchase an alternative-fuel sweeper. (SR 
1186.1-5; emphasis added.) 
. 
. 
* 
PR 1186.1 requires that diesel street sweepers 
be replaced with alternative-fuel street sweepers 
after July 
1, 2001. (SR 1186.1-11; emphasis 
added.) 
63. 
The purpose of Rule 1186.1 is to effect a general 
quantitative reduction in emissions from street sweeper 
vehicles and engines operating in the District. 
64. 
Rule 
1186.1 
establishes 
emissions-related 
re- 
quirements as conditions precedent to the initial purchase 
and sale of certain new motor vehicles or motor vehicle 
engines. 
65. 
Rule 
1186.1 
prohibits 
or 
limits, 
directly 
or 
indirectly, the purchase and sale of certain new motor 
vehicles and motor vehicle engines (i.e. diesel-fueled street 
sweepers) that are certified 
in California 
as meeting 
California standards. 


[PDF page 102]
181 
G. Rule 1196 
66. 
Rule 1196, entitled “Clean On-Road Heavy-Duty 
Public Fleet Vehicles,” was adopted on October 20, 2000. A 
copy of Rule 1196 is appended hereto as Exhibit “K.” 
67. 
The stated purpose of Rule 1196 (as set forth in 
paragraph (a) of the Rule) is to “require public fleet opera- 
tors to acquire alternative-fuel heavy-duty vehicles when 
procuring or leasing these vehicles to reduce air toxic and 
criteria pollutant emissions.” (Rule 1196(a); emphasis added.) 
68. 
Paragraph (c\(1) of Rule 1196 defines “alterna- 
tive-fuel heavy-duty engine or vehicle” to mean “a heavy- 
duty vehicle or engine that uses compressed or liquefied 
natural gas, propane, methanol, electricity, fuel cells, or 
other advanced technologies that do not rely on diesel fuel, 
and has been certified by the California Air Resources 
Board (CARB).” (Rule 1196(c)\1); emphasis added.) 
69. 
Paragraph (d) of Rule 1196 provides that begin- 
ning July 1, 2002, unless a fleet operator obtains a “Tech- 
nical 
Infeasibility 
Certification” 
from 
the SCAQMD’s 
Executive Officer, all new additions to an existing fleet, or 
formation of a new fleet, or heavy-duty vehicles, shall be 
by purchase or lease of: (1) alternative-fuel heavy-duty 
engines or vehicles; (2) dual-fuel heavy-duty vehicles; or 
(3) dedicated gasoline heavy-duty vehicles. (Rule 1196(d).) 
70. A copy of the final SCAQMD Staff Report for 
Rule 1196 is appended hereto as Exhibit “L.” The Staff 
Report explains the purpose of Rule 1196, as follows: 
One of the primary objectives of the proposed 
Clean Fleet program, which includes PR 1196, is 
to reduce TAC emissions, primarily diesel par- 
ticulate, by accelerating the implementation of 
182 
commercially available alternative-fuel engines. 
(SR 1196-3). 
. 
* 
* 
The purpose of PR 1196 is to increase the 
number of alternative-fuel or gasoline heavy- 
duty vehicles used by fleet operators providing 
public services in the AQMD’s jurisdiction to re- 
duce air toxic and criteria pollutant emissions. 
(SR 1196-6.) 
* 
* 
* 
With regard to criteria pollutants, PR — is 
specifically based on achieving emission reduc- 
tions beyond the mandatory U.S. EPA and ARB 
heavy-duty engine emission standards. (SR 1196- 
12; emphasis added.) 
71. 
The Staff Report for Rule 1196 confirms that the 
purchase and sale of certain ARB-certified heavy-duty 
vehicles and engines will be prohibited or limited under 
the Rule. Specifically, the Staff Report states, 
Proposed Rule (PR) 1196 — Clean On-Road

quire public fleet operators to purchase alterna- 
tive-fuel, 
dual-fuel, 
or

72. 
The purpose of Rule 1196 is to effect a general

185 
78. 
The foregoing provisions of the Governing Board

that the SCAQMD Fleet Rules are intended to effect

motor vehicles and motor vehicle engines. These provi-

certification or other approval requirements relating to the 
control of emissions from new motor vehicles or new motor

to the initial

Conclusions 
of Law 
79. 
This Court has original jurisdiction over this 
action pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 1331 because it arises 
under ‘the Supremacy Clause, the laws of the United 
States, and 42 U.S.C. § 1983. Venue is proper in this 
District under 28 U.S.C. § 1391(b) because the SCAQMD’s 
headquarters are located in this District, and because the 
SCAQMD Fleet Rules pertain to public fleets owned and 
— in this District. (Complaint and Answer, 7] 11- 
80. 
Section 
209%(a) 
of the 
CAA 
prohibits 
the 
SCAQMD from adopting or attempting to enforce “any 
standard relating to the control of emissions from new 
motor vehicles or new motor vehicle engines.” Section 
209%a) also prohibits the SCAQMD from requiring any 
type of “certification ... or any other approval relating to 
the control of emissions from any new motor vehicle or 
new motor vehicle as condition precedent to the initial 
retail sale .. 
. of such motor vehicle, motor vehicle engine, 
or equipment.” 42 U.S.C. §7543(a). 
186 
81. 
The statutory term “standard relating to the 
control of emissions” means a requirement the purpose of 
which is to effect a general quantitative reduction in 
emissions. See Assoc. of Intn’l Auto. Mfrs. v. Commissioner, 
208 F.3d 1, 6-7 (1st Cir. 2000); Amer. Auto. Mfrs. Assoc. v. 
Cahill, 152 F.3d 196, 200 (2nd Cir. 1998). 
82. 
The SCAQMD Fleet Rules constitute standards 
relating to the control of emissions from new motor vehi- 
cles and new motor vehicle engines. 
83. 
The SCAQMD Fleet Rules establish certification 
or other approval requirements relating to the control of 
emissions from new motor vehicles or new motor vehicle 
engines as conditions precedent to the purchase and sale 
of such motor vehicles or engines. 
84. 
Through their adoption and attempts to enforce 
the SCAQMD Fleet Rules, the SCAQMD Defendants have 
adopted and are attempting to enforce standards relating 
to the control of emissions from new motor vehicles or new 
motor vehicle engines, and the SCAQMD Defendants also 
are establishing certification of other emissions-related 
approval requirements 
as conditions precedent to the 
initial purchase and sale of new motor vehicles or new 
motor vehicle engines. 
85. 
The SCAQMD Fleet Rules violate the provisions 
of Section 209(a) of the CAA, and so are preempted and 
invalid under controlling federal law and the Supremacy 
Clause of the U.S. Constitution. U.S. Const., Art. VI, cl.2. 
86. 
Section 177 of the CAA bars the SCAQMD from 
prohibiting or limiting, directly or indirectly, the sale of a 
new motor vehicle or motor vehicle engine that is certified in 
‘California as meeting California standards. 42 U.S.C. §7507. 


[PDF page 105]
187 
87. 
Through their adoption and attempts to enforce 
the SCAQMD Fleet Rules, the SCAQMD Defendants have 
prohibited or limited, directly or indirectly, the purchase 
and sale of new motor vehicles or new motor vehicle 
engines that are certified in California as meeting Califor- 
nia standards. 
88. 
The SCAQMD Fleet Rules violate the provisions 
of Section 177 of the CAA, and so are preempted and 
invalid under controlling federal law and the Supremacy 
Clause of the U.S. Constitution. 
89. 
Permanent 
injunctive 
relief against 
any 
at- 
tempted 
enforcement 
of the preempted 
and 
invalid 
SCAQMD Fleet Rules i: warranted and should be granted.

March 23, 2001

After consideration of the papers in support of and in 
opposition 
to the Motion 
for Summary Judgment by 
Defendant South Coast Air Quality Management District 
and the oral argument of counsel, the Court determines 
that the following facts have been established as 
190 
UNCONTROVERTED FACTS 
1. 
Rule 1191 of the South Coast Air Quality Manage- 
ment District (hereinafter referred to as “SCAQMD” or “the 
District”) applies to governmental entities that own, lease or 
operate fleets of light-duty and medium-duty vehicles. 
[Appendix of Exhibits to Motion for Summary Judg- 
ment by Defendant South Coast Air Quality Man- 
agement District (hereafter “App. Exh.”) 1 at 2). 
2. Under Rule 1191, when the specified fleet opera- 
tors add or replace vehicles in their fleets, those operators 
must acquire non-diesel powered vehicles that meet the 
low-emission vehicle standard established by the Califor- 
nia Air Resources Board (“CARB”), or one of CARB’s more 
stringent emissions standards. 
[App. Exh. 1 at 3-4]. 
3. 
Rule 1192 of the SCAQMD applies to specified 
fleet operators in the District that are public entities that 
own, lease, or operate a fleet of 15 or more public transit 
vehicles or urban buses. 
[App. Exh. 2 at 38-39 ] 
4. 
Rule 1192 of the SCAQMD also applies to speci- 
fied fleet operators in the District that are private entities 
under contract with a government agency or a public 
transit agency to provide transit services. 
[App. Exh. 2 at 38-39]. 
5. Under Rule 1192, when the specified fleet opera- 
tors add or replace vehicles in their fleets, those operators 
must acquire alternative-fuel vehicles. 
[App. Exh. 2 at 39]. 


[PDF page 107]
191 
6. 
Rule 1193 of the SCAQMD applies to specified fleet 
operators that are public or private entities that own, 
lease, or operate a fleet of 15 or more solid waste collec- 
tion, transfer, or “rolloff” vehicles in the District. 
{[App. Exh. 3 at 77]. 
7. Under Rule 1193, when the specified fleet opera- 
tors add or replace vehicles in their fleets, those operators 
must acquire alternative-fuel vehicles. 
[App. Exh. 3 at 77]. 
8. 
Rule 1194 of the SCAQMD applies to specified 
fleet operators that are public or private entities that 
provide passenger transportation services in and out of 
commercial airports in the District. 
[App. Exh. 4 at 88, 90-91). 
9. 
Under Rule 1194, when the specified fleet opera- 
tors that use passenger cars or medium-duty vehicles add 
or replace vehicles in their fleets, those operators must 
purchase or lease a specified percentage of vehicles that 
meet CARB’s ultra low-emission vehicle standards or one 
of CARB’s more stringent emissions standards. 
[App. Exh. 4 at 90-91]. 
10. 
Under Rule 1994, when the specified fleet opera- 
tors that use heavy-duty vehicles add or replace vehicles 
in their fleets, those operators must purchase or lease 
alternative fuel vehicles. 
~ 
[App. Exh. 4 at 90-91). 
192 
11. 
Rule 1186.1 of the SCAQMD applies to specified 
operators in the District that own or lease at least 15 or 
more vehicles and provide street-sweeping services. 
[App. Exh. 5 at 108). 
12. 
Rule 1186.1 of the SCAQMD applies to specified 
fleet operators that are private entities under contract to 
provide street-sweeping services. 
| 
[App. Exh. 5 at 108). 
13. 
Under Rule 
1186.1, when the specified 
fleet 
operators add or replace street-sweepers in their fleets, 
those operators must purchase or lease alternative-fuel 
street-sweepers. 
[App. Exh. 5 at 108-09]. 
14. 
Rule 1196 of the SCAQMD applies to specified 
fleet operators that own or lease 15 or more heavy-duty 
vehicles. 
[App. Exh. 5 at 120-21). 
15. 
Under Rule 1196, when the specified fleet opera- 
tors add or replace heavy-duty vehicles, those operators 
must purchase or lease alternative-fuel vehicles, dual-fuel 
vehicles, or dedicated gasoline vehicles. 
[App. Exh. 5 120-21). 
16. 
Under California Health and Safety Code Section 
43153, only vehicles that CARB has certified may be sold 
in California. 
[Cal. Health & Safety Code § 43153]. 


[PDF page 108]
193 
17. 
Under Section 209 of the Clean Air Act, 42 U.S.C. 
§ 7509, the State of California may apply to the federal 
Environmental Protection Agency for a waiver of preemp- 
tion from the regulation of vehicles and vehicle engines. 
{42 U.S.C. § 7509). 
18. 
CARB has certified vehicles for purchase or lease 
that comply with Rules 1191, 1192, 1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 
1196 of the SCAQMD. 
[Declaration of Larry Irwin, { 7]. 
Based on the Foregoing Uncontroverted Facts, the 
Court now makes its 
CONCLUSIONS 
OF LAW 
1. 
Rules 1191, 1192, 1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 1196 of 
the SCAQMD do not establish or enforce standards relat- 
ing to the control of emissions from new motor vehicles or 
new motor vehicle engines within the meaning of Section 
209 of the Clean Air Act, 42 U.S.C.§ 7509. 
2. 
Rules 1191, 1192, 1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 1196 of 
the SCAQMD do not require any certification, inspection, 
or approval relating to the control of emissions from any 
new motor vehicle or new motor vehicle engine as a 
condition precedent to the initial sale, titling, or registra- 
tion of such motor vehicle, motor vehicle engine, or equip- 
ment within the meaning of Section 209 of the Clean Air 
Act, 42 U.S.C.§ 7509. 
3. Rules 1191, 1192, 1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 1196 of 
the SCAQMD do not prohibit or limit, directly or indi- 
rectly, the manufacture or sale of a new motor vehicle or 
motor vehicle engine that is certified in California as 
194 
meeting California 
standards 
within 
the meaning of 
Section 177 of the Clean Air Act, 42 U.S.C. § 7507. 
4. 
Rules 1191, 1192, 1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 1196 of 
the SCAQMD do not create, or have the effect of creating, 
a motor vehicle or motor vehicle engine different than a 
motor vehicle or engine certified 
in California under 
California standards (“a third vehicle”) or otherwise create 
such a “third vehicle” within the meaning of Section 177 of 
the Clean Air Act, 42 U.S.C. § 7507. 
5. 
Rules 1191, 1192, 1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 1196 of 
the SCAQMD are not preempted under the Supremacy 
Clause of the United States Constitution. 
6. Substantial evidence in the District’s administra- 
tive record for Rules 1191, 1192, 1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 
1196 supports the District’s conclusion that fuels such as 
methanol and natural gas are equivalently clean-burning 
alternative fuels within the meaning of California Health 
and Safety Code Section 40447.5. 
7. The District did not violate California Health and 
Safety Code Section 40447.5 in adopting Rules 1191, 1192, 
1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 1196. 
8. 
Rules 1191, 1192, 1193, 1194, 1186.1, and 1196 of 
the SCAQMD were lawfully enacted and are lawful and 
valid in all respects. 


[PDF page 109]
195

I, John W. Duerr, declare as follows: 
1. 
I am employed by Detroit Diesel Corporation as 
Manager, Regulatory Activities. I have personal know]- 
edge of the facts stated in this declaration, except those 
facts stated on information and belief, and if called to 
testify as a witness to such matters under oath, I could 
and would competently testify thereto. As to those matters 
stated upon information and belief, I am informed and 
believe and on that basis declare such facts are true to the 
best of my knowledge. 
2. 
Detroit Diesel Corporation (“DDC”) is a member of 
the Engine Manufacturers Association. DDC makes many 
models of internal combustion engines, including various 
compression-ignition 
diesel-fueled 
engines 
for use 
in 
several types of heavy-duty on-highway motor vehicles 
such as trucks and buses. 
3. 
Before 
a new 
heavy-duty 
diesel-fueled 
motor 
vehicle engine may be sold in the United States, it must be 
certified by the United States Environmental Protection 
Agency (“EPA”) as meeting all applicable EPA emission- 
control regulations and standards. As part of the on-highway 
engine certification process, DDC submits detailed engine 
198 
: 
emission testing data and other information to demon- 
strate that an on-highway engine model complies with the 
applicable 
engine 
emission 
standards, 
including 
the 
emission standards for oxides of nitrogen, hydrocarbons, 
— 
carbon monoxide, and particulate matter emissions. 
If, 
based on the submitted information, EPA is satisfied that 
an on-highway engine model meets the applicable emis- 
sion standards, EPA will issue a certificate that certifies 
the engine model for sale during the next model year. 
4. 
California has its own emission standards appli- 
cable to new heavy-duty on-highway diesel-fueled motor 
vehicle engines sold into California. Accordingly, DDC 
follows a similar process of submitting engine testing data 
and other information to the California Air Resources 
Board (“ARB”) to obtain engine certification. Like EPA, if 
the ARB is satisfied, based on the information submitted, 
that an on-highway engine model satisfies the applicable 
ARB emission standards and requirements, then the ARB 
will issue an Executive Order approving the sale of the 
heavy-duty on-highway engine model during the next 
model year. 
5. DDC has followed the required data submittal 
process and has received certification from ARB and EPA 
for the sale of various models of on-highway diesel-fueled 
engines during the 2001 model year. A schedule of DDC’s 
heavy-duty on-highway diesel-fueled engines that the ARB 
has certified for sale in California for the 2001 model year 
is appended to this declaration as Exhibit 1. 
6. Upon information and belief, it is my understand- 
ing that the South Coast Air quality Management District 
(“SCAQMD”) has adopted a series of rules (the “SCAQMD 
Fleet Rules”) that prohibit or limit certain local fleet 


[PDF page 111]
199 
operators from acquiring on-highway diesel-fueled motor 
vehicles 
and engines. 
Based 
on 
that understanding, 
Exhibit 1 lists the on-highway diesel-fueled motor vehicle 
engine models that DDC manufactures, and that are 
currently certified by the ARB for sale in California, the 
purchase and sale of which is prohibited or limited under 
the SCAQMD Fleet Rules. 
I declare under penalty of perjury under the laws of 
the State of California that the foregoing is true and 
correct. Executed this 23rd day of March, 2001.

I, Eugene M. Sweeney, declare as follows: 
1. 
I am employed by Caterpillar Inc. as Manager of 
Technical Support. I have personal knowledge of the facts 
stated in this declaration, except those facts stated on 
information and belief, and if called to testify as a witness 
to such matters under oath, I could and would competently 
testify thereto. As to those matters stated upon informa- 
tion and belief, I am informed and believe and on that 
basis declare such facts are true to the best of my knowl- 
edge. 
2. 
Caterpillar Inc. (“Caterpillar”) is a member of the 
Engine 
Manufacturers 
Association. 
Caterpillar 
makes 
many models of internal combustion engines, including 
various compression-ignition diesel-fueled engines for use 
in several types of on-highway motor vehicles, such as 
vans, trucks and buses. 
} 
3. 
Before 
a new on-highway 
diesel-fueled 
motor 
vehicle engine may be sold in the United States, it must be 
certified by the United States Environmental Protection 
Agency (“EPA”) as meeting all applicable EPA emission- 
control regulations and standards. As part of the on- 
highway engine certification process, Caterpillar submits 
—_— 
-- 
203 
detailed engine emission testing data to demonstrate that 
an on-highway engine model complies with the applicable 
engine emission standards, including the emission stan- 
dards for oxides of nitrogen, hydrocarbons, carbon monox- 
ide, and particulate matter emissions. If, based on the 
submitted data, EPA is satisfied that an on-highway 
engine model meets the applicable emission standards, 
EPA will issue a certificate that certifies the engine model 
for sale during the next model year. 
4. 
California has its own emission standards appli- 
cable 
to new on-highway 
diesel-fueled 
motor 
vehicle 
engines 
sold 
into 
California. Accordingly, 
Caterpillar 
follows a similar process of submitting engine testing data 
to the California Air Resources Board (“CARB”) to obtain 
engine certification. Like EPA, if the CARB is satisfied, 
based on the engine test data submitted, that an on- 
highway engine model 
satisfies 
the applicable CARB 
emission standards and requirements, then the CARB will 
issue an Executive Order approving the sale of the on- 
highway engine model during the next model year. 
5. 
Caterpillar has followed the required data submit- 
tal process and has received certification from CARB and 
EPA for the sale of various models of on-highway diesel- 
fueled engines during the 2001 model year. A schedule of 
Caterpillar’s on-highway diesel-fueled engines that the 
CARB has certified for sale in California for the 2001 
model year is appended to this declaration as Exhibit 1. 
6. Upon information and belief, it is my understand- 
ing that the South Coast Air quality Management District 
(“SCAQMD”) has adopted a series of rules (the “SCAQMD 
Fleet Rules”) that prohibit or limit certain local fleet 
operators from acquiring on-highway diesel-fueled motor 


[PDF page 115]
204 
vehicles 
and engines. 
Based 
on 
that understanding, 
Exhibit 1 lists the on-highway diesel-fueled motor vehicle 
engine models that Caterpillar manufactures, and that are 
currently certified by the CARB for sale in California, the 
purchase and sale of which is prohibited or limited under 
the SCAQMD Fleet Rules. 
I declare under penalty of perjury under the laws of 
the State of California that the foregoing is true and 
correct. Executed this 22nd day of March, 2001. 
/s/ Eugene 
M. Sweeney

I, Steven R. Butler, declare as follows: 
1. 
I am employed by Cummins Inc. as an Engine 
Certification Director. I have personal knowledge of the 
facts stated in this declaration, except those facts stated 
on information and belief, and if called to testify as a

upon information and belief, I am informed and believe 
and on that basis declare such facts are true to the best 
of my knowledge. 
2. Cummins Inc. (“Cummins”) is a member of the 
Engine Manufacturers 
Association. 
Cummins 
makes 
many models of internal combustion engines, including 
various compression-ignition diesel-fueled engines for 
use in several types of on-highway motor vehicles, such 
as vans, trucks and buses. 
3. 
Before a new diesel-fueled motor vehicle engine 
may be sold in the United States, it must be certified by 
the United States Environmental 
Protection Agency 
(“EPA”) as meeting all applicable EPA emission-control 
regulations and standards. As part of the on-highway 
engine certification process, Cummins submits detailed 
208 
engine emission testing data to demonstrate that an on- 
highway engine model complies with the applicable 
engine 
emission 
standards, 
including 
the emission 
standards for oxides of nitrogen, hydrocarbons, carbon 
monoxide, and particulate matter emissions. If, based on 
the submitted data, EPA is satisfied that an on-highway 
engine model meets the applicable emission standards, 
EPA will issue a certificate that certifies the engine 
model for sale during the next model year. 
4. 
California 
has 
its own 
emission 
standards 
applicable to new on-highway diesel-fueled motor vehi- 
cle engines sold into California. Accordingly, Cummins 
follows a similar process of submitting engine testing 
data to the California Air Resources Board (“ARB”) to 
obtain engine certification. Like EPA, if the ARB is 
satisfied, based on the engine test data submitted, that 
an on-highway engine model satisfies the applicable 
ARB emission standards and requirements, then the 
ARB will issue an Executive Order approving the sale of 
the on-highway engine model during the next model 
year. 
- 
5. Cummins has followed the required data submit- 
tal process and has received certification from ARB and 
EPA for the sale of various models of on-highway diesel- 
fueled engines during the 2001 model year. A schedule of 
Cummins’ on-highway diesel-fueled engines that the ARB 
has certified for sale in California for the 2001 model year 
is appended to this declaration as Exhibit 1.

I, Jeffrey A. Marsce, declare as follows: 
1. 
I am employed by Isuzu Motors America, Inc. as 
Manager of Vehicle Compliance. I have personal knowl- 
edge of the facts stated in this declaration, except those 
facts stated on information and belief, and if called to _

and would competently

are true to the best of my knowledge. 
2. 
Isuzu Motors America, Inc. (“Isuzu”) is a mem- 
ber of the Engine Manufacturers Association. Isuzu is 
involved in the certification process for many models of 
internal combustion engines, including various compres- 
sion-ignition diesel-fueled engines for use in several 
types of on-highway motor vehicles, such as vans, trucks 
and buses. 
ee 
eee 
213 
3. 
Before a new diesel-fueled motor vehicle engine 
may be sold in the United States, it must be certified by 
the United States Environmental Protection Agency 
(“EPA”) as meeting all applicable EPA emission-control 
regulations and standards. As part of the on-highway 
engine certification 
process, 
Isuzu 
submits 
detailed 
engine emission testing data to demonstrate that an on- 
highway engine model complies with the applicable 
engine 
emission 
standards, 
imcluding 
the emission 
standards for oxides of nitrogem, hydrocarbons, carbon 
monoxide, and particulate matter emissions. If, based on 
the submitted data, EPA is satisfied that an on-highway 
engine model meets the applicable emission standards, 
EPA will issue a certificate that certifies the engine 
model for sale during the next model year. 
4. 
California 
has 
its own 
emission 
standards 
applicable to new on-highway diesel-fueled motor vehi- 
cle engines sold into California. 
Accordingly, 
Isuzu 
follows a similar process of submitting engine testing 
data to the California Air Resources Board (“ARB”) to 
obtain engine certification. Like EPA, if the ARB is 
satisfied, based on the engine test data submitted, that 
an on-highway engine model satisfies the applicable 
ARB emission standards and requirements, then the 
ARB will issue an Executive Order approving the sale of 
the on-highway engine model during the next model 
year.

[PDF page 123]

ARB has certified for sale in California for the 2001

or limited under the SCAQMD Fleet Rules.

I, Larry Irwin, declare: 
l. 
I am an Air Quality Specialist in the Planning 
Division of the South Coast Air Quality Management 
District 
with 
responsibility 
for mobile 
source 
rule 
implementation. For the past ten years, my primary 
duties have been mobile source rule development and 
implementation. Among my job responsibilities 
are 
monitoring the development and use of alternative fuel 


[PDF page 125]
218 
vehicles and the availability of alternative fuel vehicles. 
I have a Bachelor of Science degree in physics from Ohio 
State University. 
2. The California Air Resources Board (“CARB”) 
has the responsibility for regulating emissions of air 
pollutants from motor vehicles operated in California. 
CARB 
accordingly 
administers 
exhaust 
emissions 
standards for various classes of new motor vehir ie- 
passenger cars, light-duty trucks, medium-duty vehicles, 
and engines used in heavy-duty vehicles. 
3. 
In 1990-1991, CARB adopted 
the California 
Low-Emission Vehicle (LEV) regulations for cars, light- 
duty trucks, and medium-duty vehicles. Every new car, 
light-duty truck, or medium-duty vehicle offered for sale 
in California must be certified by CARB as complying 
with the LEV regulations. The LEV regulations consist 
of tailpipe emissions standards and four increasingly 
stringent, low-emission vehicle categories to which each 
vehicle must be certified. The categories are Transi- 
tional Low-Emission 
Vehicle, Low-Emission 
Vehicle, 
Ultra-Low-Emission Vehicle, and Zero-Emission Vehi- 
cle. The LEV regulations also require that for each 
model year between 1994 and 2003, manufacturers must 
identify the average emissions 
level of non-methane 
organic gases (NMOG) to which its fleet of cars and light 
trucks was certified, and then, on certain target dates, 
meet an incrementally more stringent fleet average 
NMOG level for its fleet. 
4. CARB 
also 
has 
adopted 
exhaust 
emissions 
standards 
for 
heavy-duty 
engines. 
All 
heavy-duty 
engines offered for sale in California must meet CARB’s 
exhaust emissions standards. 
219 
5. The Fleet Rules are expressly designed to work 
in tandem with CARB’s certification process. Each of the 
Fleet Rules requires fleet operators to purchase certified 
vehicles in the lower-polluting categories. The vehicles 
meeting the Fleet Rules requirements are currently 
available and certified for use in California. In fact, as a 
result of the production component of CARB’s rules, 
manufacturers must produce some vehicles in some of 
the cleaner emissions classes for sale in California. 
6. 
The Fleet Rules rely on CARB’s determinations 
and requirements regarding vehicle emission levels. The 
purpose of the Fleet Rules is to require fleet operators to 
purchase the lower-emitting certified vehicles when they 
are available. 
If, for some unexpected reason, lower- 
polluting vehicles are unavailable for purchase from 
dealers or from engine and vehicle manufacturers, the 
Fleet Rules allow fleet operators to purchase higher- 
polluting certified vehicles. 
7. Attached to my declaration is a list of vehicles 
that CARB has certified and that meet the requirements 
of the Fleet Rules. The list is broken down into catego- 
ries corresponding to each of the individual Fleet Rules. 
I compiled the list by examining (1) vehicle data sup- 
plied to me by CARB, and (2) executive orders issued by 
CARB that specify CARB’s emissions certifications. 
I declare under penalty of perjury under the laws of 
the State of California that the foregoing is true and 
correct.

CHAPTER 3 
EXISTING SETTING 
Introduction 
Air Quality 
Water Resources 
Transportation / Circulation 
Public Services
